Tree-Packing Revisited: Faster Fully Dynamic Min-Cut and Arboricity
Authors: Tijn de Vos, Aleksander Christiansen
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Tree-packings – collections of spanning trees of a graph – are a fundamental tool in the study of minimum cut and related graph parameters. They have played a central role in the design of algorithms across static, dynamic, and distributed settings. In this paper, we study both tree-packings themselves and their structural connections to min-cut and arboricity. Our results lead to faster dynamic algorithms for both problems. For dynamic min-cut, [Thorup, Comb. 2007] used tree-packings to obtain his dynamic min-cut algorithm with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\lambda ^{14.5}\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(λ14.5n) worst-case update time. We reexamine this relationship, showing that we need to maintain fewer trees for such a result; we show that we only need to pack \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta (\lambda ^3 \log m)$$\end{document}Θ(λ3logm) greedy trees to guarantee either a 1-respecting cut or a trivial cut in some contracted graph. Based on this structural result, we then provide a deterministic algorithm for fully dynamic exact min-cut that has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\lambda ^{5.5}\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(λ5.5n) worst-case update time, for graphs with min-cut value at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ. In particular, this also yields an algorithm for fully dynamic exact min-cut with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m^{1-1/12})$$\end{document}O~(m1-1/12) amortized update time, improving upon \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m^{1-1/31})$$\end{document}O~(m1-1/31) [Goranci et al., SODA 2023]. We also give the first fully dynamic algorithm that maintains a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation of the fractional arboricity. Our algorithm is deterministic and has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\alpha \log ^6m/\varepsilon ^4)$$\end{document}O(αlog6m/ε4) amortized update time, for graphs with arboricity at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α. We extend these results to a Monte Carlo algorithm with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\operatorname {poly}(\log m,\varepsilon ^{-1}))$$\end{document}O(poly(logm,ε-1)) amortized update time against an adaptive adversary. Our algorithms work on multi-graphs as well. Our structural results on tree-packing also include a lower bound for greedy tree-packing, which – to the best of our knowledge – is the first progress on this topic since [Thorup, Comb. 2007].

Introduction

A tree-packing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T is a collection of spanning trees of a graph. Often one is interested in a tree-packing satisfying certain requirements, e.g., greedy, disjoint, which we detail where applicable. In particular, tree-packing first appeared in the seminal works of Nash-Williams [1] and Tutte [2]. They studied sufficient and necessary conditions for when a graph can be decomposed into k disjoint spanning subgraphs, i.e., when a graph admits a disjoint tree-packing of size k. They provided a sufficient and necessary condition by considering partition values: given a partition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}P of the vertex set of a graph G, the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}P is then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tfrac{|E(G/\mathcal {P})|}{|V(G/\mathcal {P})|-1}$$\end{document}|E(G/P)||V(G/P)|-1 , where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G/\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}G/P is the graph obtained by contracting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}P in G. In particular, they showed that G admits a decomposition into k disjoint spanning trees if and only if the minimum partition value is at least k. Not long after, Nash-Williams noted [3] that his techniques extend to answer a question with a very similar flavor: what is the smallest number of trees needed to cover all edges of a graph? This number is now known as the arboricity of the graph, and Nash-Williams showed that the arboricity is the ceiling of the fractional arboricity defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha := \max _{S\subseteq V} \frac{|E(S)|}{|S|-1}$$\end{document}α:=maxS⊆V|E(S)||S|-1.

Subsequently, tree-packing has also been studied for its algorithmic applications, in particular for computing the value of a minimum cut and related problems [4–13]. The minimum cut value of a graph is defined as the minimum number of edges whose deletion causes the graph to become disconnected. Such a collection of edges forms a minimum cut or min-cut for short1. In many of these applications, ideal relative loads play a central role. The ideal relative loads (formally defined in Section 1.1) correspond to some sort of ‘ideal tree-packing’. We are still far from fully understanding ideal relative loads and their limits. In this paper, we hope to contribute to a better understanding. In particular, we study ideal relative loads by giving new upper and lower bounds on the size of tree-packings that approximate the ideal relative loads. Moreover, we show two structural results on ideal relative loads and min-cut/arboricity respectively. This leads to a faster fully dynamic exact min-cut algorithm and the first fully dynamic algorithm to maintain a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation of the fractional arboricity. As such, we categorize our results in three parts: 1) tree-packings and ideal relative loads, 2) min-cut, and 3) arboricity.

Tree-Packings and Ideal Relative Loads

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E) be an unweighted undirected graph. A tree-packing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T of G is a family of spanning trees, where edges can appear in multiple trees. The load of an edge e, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{\mathcal {T}}(e)$$\end{document}LT(e), is defined by the number of trees that contain e. The relative load is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{\mathcal {T}}(e) = L^{\mathcal {T}}(e)/|\mathcal {T}|$$\end{document}ℓT(e)=LT(e)/|T|. Whenever the tree-packing is clear from the context, we omit the superscript \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cdot ^{\mathcal {T}}$$\end{document}·T. The packing value of a tree-packing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathrm {pack\_val}(\mathcal {T}) := \frac{1}{\max _{e\in E} \ell ^{\mathcal {T}}(e)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pack_val(T):=1maxe∈EℓT(e).Dual to tree-packing we have a concept for partitions. For a partition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}$$\end{document}P of the vertex set V, we define the partition value as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathrm {part\_val}(\mathcal {P}) := \frac{|E(G/\mathcal {P})|}{|\mathcal {P}|-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}part_val(P):=|E(G/P)||P|-1.We now have that (see e.g. [1, 2])\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _G:= \max _{\mathcal {T}} \mathrm {pack\_val}(\mathcal {T}) = \min _{\mathcal {P}} \mathrm {part\_val}(\mathcal {P}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΦG:=maxTpack_val(T)=minPpart_val(P).We omit the subscript and simply write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ when the graph is clear from the context.

Next, we introduce ideal relative loads, following Thorup [8]. These loads, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^*(e)$$\end{document}ℓ∗(e), are defined recursively. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}^*$$\end{document}P∗ be a partition with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {part\_val}(\mathcal {P}^*)=\Phi $$\end{document}part_val(P∗)=Φ.For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in E(G/\mathcal {P}^*)$$\end{document}e∈E(G/P∗), set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^*(e):= 1/\Phi $$\end{document}ℓ∗(e):=1/Φ.For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\in \mathcal {P}^*$$\end{document}S∈P∗, recurse on the subgraph G[S].By definition, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _G =\tfrac{1}{\max _{e\in E}\ell ^*(e)}$$\end{document}ΦG=1maxe∈Eℓ∗(e). We show an analogous result for the fractional arboricity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha (G)$$\end{document}α(G) and the minimum ideal relative load:

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}^*$$\end{document}P∗ be a partition with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {part\_val}(\mathcal {P}^*)=\Phi $$\end{document}part_val(P∗)=Φ.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}^*$$\end{document}P∗ be a partition with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {part\_val}(\mathcal {P}^*)=\Phi $$\end{document}part_val(P∗)=Φ.

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in E(G/\mathcal {P}^*)$$\end{document}e∈E(G/P∗), set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^*(e):= 1/\Phi $$\end{document}ℓ∗(e):=1/Φ.

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in E(G/\mathcal {P}^*)$$\end{document}e∈E(G/P∗), set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^*(e):= 1/\Phi $$\end{document}ℓ∗(e):=1/Φ.

For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\in \mathcal {P}^*$$\end{document}S∈P∗, recurse on the subgraph G[S].

For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\in \mathcal {P}^*$$\end{document}S∈P∗, recurse on the subgraph G[S].

Theorem 1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E) be an undirected, unweighted (multi-)graph, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha (G) = \frac{1}{\min _{e\in E} \ell ^{*}(e)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α(G)=1mine∈Eℓ∗(e).

In Section 1.3, we demonstrate how this leads to novel dynamic algorithms to approximate the fractional arboricity.

We obtain a second structural result, which regards tree-packing and the min-cut. We see a first connection between these two concepts as follows: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ denote the min-cut of G, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda /2 < \Phi \le \lambda $$\end{document}λ/2<Φ≤λ [5]. Combining this fact with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi =\tfrac{1}{\max _{e\in E}\ell ^*(e)}$$\end{document}Φ=1maxe∈Eℓ∗(e), we see that the min-cut relates to the ideal relative loads. We investigate this relation further in Section 1.2, where we state our somewhat more technical result.

Before we discuss these two structural results, we consider how to approximately compute ideal relative loads.

Approximating Ideal Relative loads.

Computing the ideal relative loads exactly seems to be a challenging question. Giving a tree-packing such that its relative loads approximate the ideal relative loads seems easier. More precisely, the goal is to find a tree-packing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T such that1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\ell ^\mathcal {T}(e)-\ell ^*(e)| \le \varepsilon /\lambda , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ℓT(e)-ℓ∗(e)|≤ε/λ,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in E$$\end{document}e∈E. In applications, we need for algorithmic efficiency that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T is small. In general, such a tree-packing needs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda /\varepsilon $$\end{document}λ/ε trees. We prove that there always exists a tree-packing of this size.

Theorem 2

Let G be an unweighted, undirected (multi-)graph. There exists a tree-packing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathcal {T}|=\Theta (\lambda /\varepsilon )$$\end{document}|T|=Θ(λ/ε) trees that satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\ell ^\mathcal {T}(e)-\ell ^*(e)| \le \varepsilon /\lambda , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ℓT(e)-ℓ∗(e)|≤ε/λ,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in E$$\end{document}e∈E.

Although our algorithm is constructive, it is not efficient. Instead, we consider greedy tree-packings.

Greedy Tree-Packing.

Thorup [8] showed that a greedy tree-packing approximates the ideal packing well. Here a greedy tree-packing is defined as follows: let the weight of an edge be the number of trees an edge belongs to, and require that the spanning trees in the packing form successive minimum weight spanning trees. Thorup showed that a greedy tree-packing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathcal {T}|\ge 6\lambda \log m/\varepsilon ^2$$\end{document}|T|≥6λlogm/ε2 trees2 satisfies Equation 1. The fact that greedy tree-packings approximate ideal packings will be the basis for our dynamic min-cut and arboricity algorithms, since greedy tree-packings are easy to compute – even in a dynamic setting.

To make any algorithm based on Equation 1 as efficient as possible, it is worth to study how many greedy trees are necessary to attain it. We establish a novel lower bound.

Theorem 3

Let G be an unweighted, undirected (multi-)graph. In general, a greedy tree-packing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T needs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathcal {T}|=\Omega (\lambda /\varepsilon ^{{3}/{2}})$$\end{document}|T|=Ω(λ/ε3/2) trees to satisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\ell ^\mathcal {T}(e)-\ell ^*(e)| \le \varepsilon /\lambda , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ℓT(e)-ℓ∗(e)|≤ε/λ,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in E$$\end{document}e∈E, whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^{-1} = O(n^{1/3})$$\end{document}ε-1=O(n1/3).

Although a greedy tree-packing is easy to compute and maintain, this lower bound combined with Theorem 2 indicate that investigating other tree-packings might be worthwhile.

Min-Cut

In a seminal paper, Gabow [4] showed how to compute both a packing of disjoint trees and a min-cut. Subsequently, Karger [5] used the fact that any large enough greedy tree-packing contains trees that 2-respect some min-cut, i.e., it contains a spanning tree which crosses some min-cut at most twice. To arrive at his near-linear time min-cut algorithm for computing a min-cut, Karger used this fact by showing that one can efficiently compute the size of all cuts that 2-respects a tree. This observation has also been proven useful for computing k-cuts [9, 10].

This ‘semi-duality’ between tree-packing and min-cut has additionally found applications in other models of computation such as distributed computing [11, 13] and dynamic algorithms [6, 8]. Dynamic algorithms maintain a solution to a problem – for instance the size of a min-cut – as the input graph undergoes deletions and insertions of edges. In this setting, it is not known how to maintain the smallest 2-respecting cut in a dynamic forest. Instead, Thorup [8] showed that packing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathcal {T}|=\Omega (\lambda ^7 \log ^{3} m)$$\end{document}|T|=Ω(λ7log3m) greedy trees is sufficient to guarantee that at least one tree in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T 1-respects a min-cut, provided that the size of the min-cut is at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ. He then shows that one can dynamically maintain this packing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(|\mathcal {T}|^2 \sqrt{\lambda n})$$\end{document}O~(|T|2λn) update time3 and that one can maintain the smallest 1-respecting cut efficiently in a dynamic forest. These things combine to give an exact dynamic min-cut algorithm running in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\lambda ^{14.5}\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(λ14.5n) worst-case update time, whenever the min-cut has size at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ.

This dependency on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ is persistent. Even the cases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda = 1$$\end{document}λ=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda = 2$$\end{document}λ=2 are important and very well-studied in the dynamic setting [14–25]. Recently Jin, Sun, and Thorup [26] gave an algorithm for the case that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \le (\log m)^{o(1)}$$\end{document}λ≤(logm)o(1). For general (polynomial) values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ, Thorup [8] remains the state-of-the-art. One immediate way of improving the dependency on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ in his update time is to show that packing even fewer trees still guarantees that at least one tree 1-respects a min-cut. This prompted Thorup to ask if it is possible to show that an even smaller tree-packing always 1-respects at least one min-cut.

Our Structural Result.

In this paper, we show that a better \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ dependency is possible. We do not attain this by improving directly on Thorup’s bound that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (\lambda ^7 \log m)$$\end{document}Ω(λ7logm) greedy trees suffice. Instead, we find that in the dynamic setting, focusing exclusively on 1-respecting cuts may be too limiting. In particular, we prove that in the cases where one might need to pack many trees to ensure that at least one packed tree 1-respects a min-cut, one can instead consider a corresponding approximate partition. We show that whenever we cannot guarantee a 1-respecting min-cut, we can instead guarantee that the approximate partition contains a trivial min-cut. Here, a trivial min-cut is a cut where one side consists of a single vertex. With this approach, we need to pack only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega (\lambda ^3 \log m)$$\end{document}Ω(λ3logm) trees, thus resulting in a much smaller final dependency on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ.

Theorem 4

Let G be an unweighted, undirected (multi-)graph, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T be a greedy tree-packing on G. Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathcal {T}|=\Omega (\lambda ^3\log m)$$\end{document}|T|=Ω(λ3logm), then at least one of the following holds Some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T∈T 1-respects a min-cut of G; orSome trivial cut in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G/ \{e\in E: \ell ^\mathcal {T}(e)<\tfrac{2}{\lambda }-\tfrac{1}{2\lambda ^2}\}$$\end{document}G/{e∈E:ℓT(e)<2λ-12λ2} corresponds to a min-cut of G.

Some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T∈T 1-respects a min-cut of G; or

Some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T∈T 1-respects a min-cut of G; or

Some trivial cut in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G/ \{e\in E: \ell ^\mathcal {T}(e)<\tfrac{2}{\lambda }-\tfrac{1}{2\lambda ^2}\}$$\end{document}G/{e∈E:ℓT(e)<2λ-12λ2} corresponds to a min-cut of G.

Some trivial cut in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G/ \{e\in E: \ell ^\mathcal {T}(e)<\tfrac{2}{\lambda }-\tfrac{1}{2\lambda ^2}\}$$\end{document}G/{e∈E:ℓT(e)<2λ-12λ2} corresponds to a min-cut of G.

Our Dynamic Results.

We combine the structural result from Theorem 4 with novel dynamic subroutines to obtain the following result.

Theorem 5

There exists a deterministic dynamic algorithm that, given an unweighted, undirected (multi-)graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E), maintains the exact min-cut value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \le \lambda _{\max }$$\end{document}λ≤λmax in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\lambda _{\max }^{5.5}\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(λmax5.5n) worst-case update time.

It can return the edges of the cut in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\lambda \log m)$$\end{document}O(λlogm) time with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\lambda _{\max }^5\sqrt{m})$$\end{document}O~(λmax5m) worst-case update time.

This improves on the state-of-the-art by Thorup [8], who achieves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\lambda _{\max }^{14.5}\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(λmax14.5n) worst-case update time. Thorup also uses this result to obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximate min-cut against an oblivious adversary in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(n) time. In this setting, our result improves the polylogarithmic factors. An application where our result has more impact is deterministic exact min-cut for unbounded min-cut value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ.

Theorem 6

There exists a deterministic dynamic algorithm that, given a simple, unweighted, undirected graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E), maintains the exact min-cut value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ with amortized update time\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \tilde{O}(\min \{m^{1-1/12}, m^{11/13}n^{1/13},n^{3/2}\}). $$\end{document}O~(min{m1-1/12,m11/13n1/13,n3/2}).It can return the edges of the cut in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\lambda \log m)$$\end{document}O(λlogm) time with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m^{1-1/12})$$\end{document}O~(m1-1/12) amortized update time.

We obtain this result by using the algorithm of Goranci et al. [27] for the high \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ regime. When they combine this with [8], they achieve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m^{29/31}n^{1/31})=\tilde{O}(m^{1-1/31})$$\end{document}O~(m29/31n1/31)=O~(m1-1/31) amortized update time4. We note that Goranci et al. [27] also provide a randomized result with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n)$$\end{document}O~(n) worst-case update time against an adaptive adversary.

Arboricity

As mentioned, the fractional arboricity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α of a graph is defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha := \max _{S\subseteq V} \frac{|E(S)|}{|S|-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α:=maxS⊆V|E(S)||S|-1.The (integral) arboricity is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lceil \alpha \rceil $$\end{document}⌈α⌉, so it is strictly easier to compute. Equivalently, we can define the arboricity as the minimum number of trees needed to cover the graph [1]. As stated in Theorem 1, we prove that the arboricity can be expressed in terms of the ideal relative loads. We use this equality to maintain an approximation of the arboricity in the dynamic setting.

Our Dynamic Results.

We obtain the first dynamic \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation of the fractional arboricity. Our algorithm is deterministic, hence also works naturally against an adaptive adversary.

Theorem 7

There exists a deterministic dynamic algorithm that, given an unweighted, undirected (multi-)graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E), maintains a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation of the fractional arboricity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \le \alpha _{\max }$$\end{document}α≤αmax in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\alpha _{\max }\log ^6 m / \varepsilon ^4)$$\end{document}O(αmaxlog6m/ε4) amortized update time or a Las Vegas algorithm with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\alpha _{\max }^2 m^{o(1)} / \varepsilon ^4)$$\end{document}O(αmax2mo(1)/ε4) worst-case update time.

This improves the approximation factor in the state-of-the-art: Chekuri et al. [28] give a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(2+ε)-approximation of the fractional arboricity. It has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log \alpha _{\max } \log ^2 m /\varepsilon ^4)$$\end{document}O(logαmaxlog2m/ε4) amortized update time or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log \alpha _{\max }\log ^3 m /\varepsilon ^6)$$\end{document}O(logαmaxlog3m/ε6) worst-case update time. In fact, for simple graphs, the value of the densest subgraph is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation for large values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α. Combining this with our result for smaller values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α, we obtain the following result.

Theorem 8

There exists a deterministic dynamic algorithm that, given a simple, unweighted, undirected graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E), maintains a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation of the fractional arboricity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log ^6 m / \varepsilon ^5)$$\end{document}O(log6m/ε5) amortized update time or a Las Vegas algorithm with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(m^{o(1)}/\varepsilon ^6)$$\end{document}O(mo(1)/ε6) worst-case update time.

For multi-graphs, the value of the densest subgraph remains a 2-approximation of the fractional arboricity, even when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α becomes large. To obtain an efficient algorithm for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation, we use a sampling technique to reduce the case of large \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α to the case of small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α. Although this is rather straight-forward against an oblivious adversary, we need to construct a more sophisticated scheme to deal with an adaptive adversary.

Theorem 9

There exists a dynamic algorithm that, given an unweighted, undirected (multi-)graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E), maintains a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation of the fractional arboricity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α against an adaptive adversary in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O( \log ^{11} m / \varepsilon ^{15})$$\end{document}O(log11m/ε15) amortized update time or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(m^{o(1)} / \varepsilon ^{19})$$\end{document}O(mo(1)/ε19) worst-case update time.

Against an oblivious adversary we obtain the improved amortized update time of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log ^{7} m /\varepsilon ^{6})$$\end{document}O(log7m/ε6), or worst-case update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(m^{o(1)}/\varepsilon ^{8})$$\end{document}O(mo(1)/ε8), see Theorem 29.

We remark that the update time of many dynamic algorithms is parameterized by the arboricity [28–34]. This shows that the arboricity is an important graph parameter. These algorithms are faster when the arboricity is small, and this is exactly the regime where dynamic approximations of the arboricity are lacking. We hope that our results contribute to a better understanding of this.

Further Related Work.

Tree-Packing.

Tree-packing can be formulated as a (packing) LP. One way of solving LPs is via the Multiplicative Weights Update (MWU) Method, see e.g., [35]. In particular, this method needs at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\rho \log m/\varepsilon ^2)$$\end{document}O(ρlogm/ε2) iterations for a packing/covering LP with width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ [36]. For tree-packing, we have width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho =\Theta (\lambda )$$\end{document}ρ=Θ(λ).

The MWU method and greedy tree-packing are strongly related. In particular, Harb, Quanrud, and Chekuri [37] note that if one uses (a particular version of) the MWU method then the resulting algorithm would also be greedy tree-packing. Chekuri, Quanrad, and Xu [12] conjecture that greedy tree-packing is in fact equivalent to approximating the LP via the standard MWU method.

We note that in both greedy tree-packing and in the MWU method, there is still freedom in the update step: in greedy tree-packing there are often multiple minimum spanning trees (e.g., the first tree can be any spanning tree) and in the MWU method we can select any of the violated constraints. For example, we could select a minimum spanning tree at random or select a violated constraint at random. Such adaptation of the standard approach might lead to better greedy tree-packing or MWU algorithms.

Tree-packing is also used for minimum k-cut [7, 12]. It is an interesting open question whether our techniques extend to this setting.

Min-Cut.

Jin and Sun [38] gave an algorithm for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s-t$$\end{document}s-t min-cut, for min-cut up to size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\log m)^{o(1)}$$\end{document}(logm)o(1) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n^{o(1)}$$\end{document}no(1) worst-case update time. Recently, they generalized these techniques to get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n^{o(1)}$$\end{document}no(1) worst-case update time for the global min-cut [26] up to size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\log m)^{o(1)}$$\end{document}(logm)o(1). There is a further line of work on smaller values of the min-cut: in particular for graph connectivity (whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ge 1$$\end{document}λ≥1) [14, 20–23, 25] and 2-edge connectivity (whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \ge 2$$\end{document}λ≥2) [15–19, 21, 24].

For planar graphs, Lacki and Sankowski [39] provided a deterministic algorithm with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{5/6})$$\end{document}O~(n5/6) worst-case update and query time.

There are faster algorithms for dynamic min-cut, at the cost of an approximation factor: Thorup and Karger [6] gave a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(2+ε)-approximation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\operatorname {poly}(\log m,\varepsilon ^{-1}))$$\end{document}O~(poly(logm,ε-1)) amortized update time, and Thorup [8] gave a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})$$\end{document}O~(n) worst-case update time.

In the partially dynamic setting, Thorup [8] gives an algorithm with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(n^{3/2}+m)$$\end{document}O~(n3/2+m) total time for the purely decremental and incremental settings. Further, there are incremental algorithms with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\lambda \log m)$$\end{document}O(λlogm) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\operatorname {poly}\log m )$$\end{document}O(polylogm) amortized update time, by Henzinger [40] and Goranci, Henzinger, and Thorup [41] respectively.

Arboricity.

Computing the (fractional) arboricity of a graph is a relatively hard problem; we do not know a static linear-time algorithm for the exact version. Gabow showed how to compute the arboricity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m^{3/2})$$\end{document}O~(m3/2) time [4, 42]. For the approximate version, much faster algorithms are known. In particular, Eppstein gave a 2-approximation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(m+n)$$\end{document}O(m+n) time [43].

Plotkin, Shmoys, and Tardos gave a FPTAS for solving fractional packing and fractional covering problems, and their algorithm applied to fractional arboricity takes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m\alpha /\varepsilon ^2)$$\end{document}O~(mα/ε2) time [36] and provides a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α denotes the value of the fractional arboricity. Toko, Worou, and Galtier gave a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation for the fractional arboricity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ O(m\log ^3 m/\varepsilon ^2)$$\end{document}O(mlog3m/ε2) time [44]. Blumenstock and Fischer [45] gave a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation of the arboricity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(m\log m\log \alpha /\varepsilon )$$\end{document}O(mlogmlogα/ε) time. More recently, Quanrud [46] gave a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation of the arboricity w.h.p. in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(m\log m\alpha (n) + n\log m(\log m + (\log \log m+\log (1/\varepsilon ))\alpha (n))/\varepsilon ^3)$$\end{document}O(mlogmα(n)+nlogm(logm+(loglogm+log(1/ε))α(n))/ε3) time, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha (n)$$\end{document}α(n) is the inverse Ackermann5.

The exact fractional arboricity can be computed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(mn)$$\end{document}O~(mn) time [42].

For dynamic arboricity, there is a deterministic exact algorithm with worst-case update time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{O}(m)$$\end{document}O~(m) [47]. The work of Brodal and Fagerberg [29] implies a 4-approximation of the arboricity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\operatorname {poly}\log m)$$\end{document}O(polylogm) amortized update time against an oblivious adversary.

Both for better approximations and for the fractional arboricity, we turn to the closely related densest subgraph problem, where we want to determine the density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \rho := \max _{S\subseteq V} \frac{|E(S)|}{|S|}. $$\end{document}ρ:=maxS⊆V|E(S)||S|.There have been multiple dynamic approximate densest subgraph algorithms over the last years [48–50]. The state-of-the-art [28] gives a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(1+ε)-approximation, which in turn imply \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2+\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(2+ε)-approximate fractional arboricity algorithms (for simple graphs). It has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log ^2 m \log \rho /\varepsilon ^4)$$\end{document}O(log2mlogρ/ε4) amortized update time or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(\log ^3 m \log \rho /\varepsilon ^6)$$\end{document}O(log3mlogρ/ε6) worst-case update time.

The algorithms via densest subgraph, but also [29], use out-orientations to compute the approximate fractional arboricity. Although this works well for crude approximations, it seems to not lead to high precision approximations.

Technical Overview

In this section, we give an overview of our techniques, starting with the results whose proofs are most extensive. However, we first define the model our dynamic results are part of.

Dynamic Graph Algorithms.

The goal is to maintain a data structure for a changing graph, that maintains the solution to some problem, e.g., the value of the min-cut or the arboricity. The input graph undergoes a series of updates, which are either edge insertions or edge deletions. If the sequence of updates is fixed from the start, we say we have an oblivious adversary. If the sequence of updates can be based upon the algorithm or the state of the data structure, we say we have an adaptive adversary. We say the algorithm has amortized update time equal to t, if it spends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma t$$\end{document}σt time for a series of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ updates. We say the algorithm has worst-case update time equal to t if it spends at most t time after every update.

In some cases, the data structure does not store the solution to the problem explicitly, but it can be retrieved by a query, e.g., in case of the edges of a min-cut. In this case we state the required time to answer such a query.

Min-Cut

To obtain our dynamic min-cut result, Theorem 5, we have three technical contributions. The first is showing Theorem 4. Our algorithm then consists of maintaining such a tree-packing, its 1-respecting cuts, and the corresponding trivial cuts. Our second contribution is showing how to maintain such trivial cuts, and the third is a new technique to decrease the recourse of tree-packing.

Min-Cut and Tree-Packing.

First, let us sketch the proof of Theorem 4. We remark that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ only differ by a constant factor, which we will use throughout.

Lemma 10

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda /2 < \Phi \le \lambda $$\end{document}λ/2<Φ≤λ.

The main observation that allows one to show that some tree 1-respects a min-cut is that if some min-cut C has\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _{e \in C} \ell ^{\mathcal {T}}(e) < 2, $$\end{document}∑e∈CℓT(e)<2,then it must be 1-respected by some tree. Indeed, then the average number of times a tree in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T crosses C is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \frac{1}{|\mathcal {T}|}\sum _{e \in C} L^{\mathcal {T}}(e) = \sum _{e \in C} \ell ^{\mathcal {T}}(e) < 2, $$\end{document}1|T|∑e∈CLT(e)=∑e∈CℓT(e)<2,so some tree must 1-respect C. Hence, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{e \in C} \ell ^{*}(e)$$\end{document}∑e∈Cℓ∗(e) is small enough, i.e., far enough below 2, then the tree-packing does not need to be very large to also concentrate the sum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{e \in C} \ell ^{\mathcal {T}}(e)$$\end{document}∑e∈CℓT(e) below 2.

Hence, we can restrict ourselves to the case where every min-cut C has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{e \in C} \ell ^{*}(e) \approx 2$$\end{document}∑e∈Cℓ∗(e)≈2. In this case, one can show that in fact every edge e participating in a min-cut has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{*}(e) \ge \bar{a} \approx \tfrac{2}{\lambda }$$\end{document}ℓ∗(e)≥a¯≈2λ for some appropriately chosen \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{a}$$\end{document}a¯. This in turn means that for a different value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{a}$$\end{document}a^ only slightly smaller than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{a}$$\end{document}a¯, the graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G/\{e \in E: \ell ^{*}(e) < \hat{a}\}$$\end{document}G/{e∈E:ℓ∗(e)<a^} must contain trivial min-cuts. To show this we assume this is not the case for contradiction, and then count the edges of the graph in two different ways: once using the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^{*}$$\end{document}ℓ∗ values and once by summing the degrees. The first way of counting implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G/\{e \in E: \ell ^{*}(e) < \hat{a}\}$$\end{document}G/{e∈E:ℓ∗(e)<a^} contains roughly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{a}^{-1} |V(G/\{e \in E: \ell ^{*}(e)< \hat{a}\})| \approx \tfrac{\lambda }{2} |V(G/\{e \in E: \ell ^{*}(e) < \hat{a}\})|$$\end{document}a^-1|V(G/{e∈E:ℓ∗(e)<a^})|≈λ2|V(G/{e∈E:ℓ∗(e)<a^})| edges, and the second way of counting shows via the Hand-Shaking Lemma that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G/\{e \in E: \ell ^{*}(e) < \hat{a}\}$$\end{document}G/{e∈E:ℓ∗(e)<a^} contains at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tfrac{\lambda +1}{2} |V(G/\{e \in E: \ell ^{*}(e) < \hat{a}\})|$$\end{document}λ+12|V(G/{e∈E:ℓ∗(e)<a^})| – a contradiction.

Now that we know a feasible \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{a}$$\end{document}a^ exists, we need to show that we do not need a very large tree packing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T to obtain the result.
